========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I just can’t sit still, and it’s getting really hard to keep pretending I can. Like, when I’m in the radiology suite or even in class, if I’m supposed to be sitting there quietly or staying in one spot, I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin. It’s not just boredom, exactly—it’s like this pressure in my body that builds up until I have to move.

I’ll be shifting around constantly, bouncing my leg, standing up when I probably shouldn’t, pacing if I can get away with it, or finding some reason to go grab something or check something just so I’m not stuck sitting there. I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.

Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t remember every single moment from being that young, obviously, but my parents still bring it up like it was this recurring thing. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. It was apparently a thing even back when I was little, like in elementary school, where sitting still and waiting my turn was already a problem.

I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t remember every single moment from being that young, obviously, but my parents still bring it up like it was this recurring thing. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. It was apparently a thing even back when I was little, like in elementary school, where sitting still and waiting my turn was already a problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yeah, I did, and I still do it now, honestly. Like, in school I’d blurt stuff out before the teacher had even finished the question, and I wouldn’t always realize I was doing it until people reacted.

Now it happens in training too. If a supervisor is explaining a positioning step, or they’re asking me something, I’ll jump in with what I think the answer is before they’re done talking. And then, a second later, I realize I missed the part that actually mattered—like they were adding a detail or correcting something specific, and I already cut them off. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.

I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t remember every single moment from being that young, obviously, but my parents still bring it up like it was this recurring thing. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. It was apparently a thing even back when I was little, like in elementary school, where sitting still and waiting my turn was already a problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did, and I still do it now, honestly. Like, in school I’d blurt stuff out before the teacher had even finished the question, and I wouldn’t always realize I was doing it until people reacted.\n\nNow it happens in training too. If a supervisor is explaining a positioning step, or they’re asking me something, I’ll jump in with what I think the answer is before they’re done talking. And then, a second later, I realize I missed the part that actually mattered—like they were adding a detail or correcting something specific, and I already cut them off. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations outside of school or work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / radiologic_technologist_or_technician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Blurting_Answers\nmanifestation: \"When a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll jump in with the answer before they finish, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, outside of school or work it happens too, but the clearest example is still when someone is explaining something to me and I jump in too fast. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.

It’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to. It’s not like I’m trying to take over the conversation, but it comes out before I’ve really stopped myself.

Yeah, I think so. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.

And then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.

I’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.

If a supervisor is explaining a positioning step, or they’re asking me something, I’ll jump in with what I think the answer is before they’re done talking. And then, a second later, I realize I missed the part that actually mattered—like they were adding a detail or correcting something specific, and I already cut them off. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations outside of school or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, outside of school or work it happens too, but the clearest example is still when someone is explaining something to me and I jump in too fast. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to. It’s not like I’m trying to take over the conversation, but it comes out before I’ve really stopped myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through in situations that don't involve talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. It was apparently a thing even back when I was little, like in elementary school, where sitting still and waiting my turn was already a problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did, and I still do it now, honestly. Like, in school I’d blurt stuff out before the teacher had even finished the question, and I wouldn’t always realize I was doing it until people reacted.\n\nNow it happens in training too. If a supervisor is explaining a positioning step, or they’re asking me something, I’ll jump in with what I think the answer is before they’re done talking. And then, a second later, I realize I missed the part that actually mattered—like they were adding a detail or correcting something specific, and I already cut them off. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations outside of school or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, outside of school or work it happens too, but the clearest example is still when someone is explaining something to me and I jump in too fast. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to. It’s not like I’m trying to take over the conversation, but it comes out before I’ve really stopped myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through in situations that don't involve talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as having a big organization system or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d say that’s the main problem.\n\nLike, with school or training stuff, I usually know what I’m supposed to do, but I’d have to think more about whether I’m bad at planning it out or managing time. I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me the way I do with interrupting or waiting my turn. It’s possible I’m missing it, but I haven’t really noticed that as much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not totally sure. I mean, my space isn’t like perfectly organized or anything, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major problem, if that makes sense.

I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I notice stuff around me like anyone does, especially if there’s a loud noise or someone walks by, but I’m not sure I get pulled off task constantly because of it.

So… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a main problem.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I just can’t sit still, and it’s getting really hard to keep pretending I can. Like, when I’m in the radiology suite or even in class, if I’m supposed to be sitting there quietly or staying in one spot, I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin. It’s not just boredom, exactly—it’s like this pressure in my body that builds up until I have to move.\n\nI’ll be shifting around constantly, bouncing my leg, standing up when I probably shouldn’t, pacing if I can get away with it, or finding some reason to go grab something or check something just so I’m not stuck sitting there. I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t remember every single moment from being that young, obviously, but my parents still bring it up like it was this recurring thing. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. Yeah… I mean, that’s actually where it’s been the biggest problem, honestly. It’s not just annoying or something people comment on casually — it’s started affecting my program in a real way.

Like, when I’m in the radiology suite or even in class, if I’m supposed to be sitting there quietly or staying in one spot, I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin. It’s not just boredom, exactly—it’s like this pressure in my body that builds up until I have to move.\n\nI’ll be shifting around constantly, bouncing my leg, standing up when I probably shouldn’t, pacing if I can get away with it, or finding some reason to go grab something or check something just so I’m not stuck sitting there. I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t remember every single moment from being that young, obviously, but my parents still bring it up like it was this recurring thing. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. It was apparently a thing even back when I was little, like in elementary school, where sitting still and waiting my turn was already a problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did, and I still do it now, honestly. Like, in school I’d blurt stuff out before the teacher had even finished the question, and I wouldn’t always realize I was doing it until people reacted.\n\nNow it happens in training too. If a supervisor is explaining a positioning step, or they’re asking me something, I’ll jump in with what I think the answer is before they’re done talking. And then, a second later, I realize I missed the part that actually mattered—like they were adding a detail or correcting something specific, and I already cut them off. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations outside of school or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, outside of school or work it happens too, but the clearest example is still when someone is explaining something to me and I jump in too fast. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to. It’s not like I’m trying to take over the conversation, but it comes out before I’ve really stopped myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through in situations that don't involve talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as having a big organization system or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d say that’s the main problem.\n\nLike, with school or training stuff, I usually know what I’m supposed to do, but I’d have to think more about whether I’m bad at planning it out or managing time. I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me the way I do with interrupting or waiting my turn. It’s possible I’m missing it, but I haven’t really noticed that as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, my space isn’t like perfectly organized or anything, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major problem, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, my room can get messy in a normal way, and I’ll have clothes or school stuff around sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example where it’s caused some big issue. With a workspace, I guess I try to keep the important stuff where it belongs, especially if other people are using the area too. I don’t really notice myself constantly losing things because of a messy space or being unable to function because of it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe a little messy sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of keeping my living space or workspace organized as one of the main things I struggle with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I notice stuff around me like anyone does, especially if there’s a loud noise or someone walks by, but I’m not sure I get pulled off task constantly because of it.\n\nLike, in class or in lab, there are always people moving around, equipment sounds, conversations happening, and I might look over for a second, but I don’t have a clear example where I’m like, “Oh yeah, that completely derailed me all the time.” I guess I’d need to think about it more, because sometimes it’s hard to tell what’s normal distraction and what’s more than that.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like acting on a whim without thinking, when you're alone or not interacting with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when you say “impulsively,” I think of stuff with other people more, like blurting things out or jumping into something socially. When I’m by myself, it’s harder for me to tell what counts.\n\nI mean, I’ll sometimes do things just because the idea pops into my head, like changing what I’m doing suddenly or deciding I’m going to do something right then instead of later, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or if that’s just normal. I don’t have a specific example where I’m like, “Yeah, I constantly act on a whim alone and it causes problems.” It’s not something I’ve really tracked, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t confidently say it happens often when I’m alone. I’d probably need you to give me examples of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as difficulty sitting still or interrupting others, caused you any significant problems in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s actually where it’s been the biggest problem, honestly. It’s not just annoying or something people comment on casually — it’s started affecting my program in a real way.\n\nMy clinical evaluations have gone down, and my supervisors have told me that the way I act during clinicals can disrupt the flow with patients and make teamwork harder. That was kind of hard to hear, because I don’t always realize in the moment how much I’m affecting the room or the pace of things. But it’s gotten serious enough that I was put on a formal remediation plan.\n\nAnd I’m pretty worried about it, because it feels like if I don’t get it under control, it could actually cost me my spot in the program. That’s probably the part that scares me most — it’s not just, “Oh, I need to be more professional.” It feels like this could mess up what I’m trying to do career-wise.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as having a big organization system or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d say that’s the main problem.\n\nLike, with school or training stuff, I usually know what I’m supposed to do, but I’d have to think more about whether I’m bad at planning it out or managing time. I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me the way I do with interrupting or waiting my turn. It’s possible I’m missing it, but I haven’t really noticed that as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, my space isn’t like perfectly organized or anything, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major problem, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, my room can get messy in a normal way, and I’ll have clothes or school stuff around sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example where it’s caused some big issue. With a workspace, I guess I try to keep the important stuff where it belongs, especially if other people are using the area too. I don’t really notice myself constantly losing things because of a messy space or being unable to function because of it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe a little messy sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of keeping my living space or workspace organized as one of the main things I struggle with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I notice stuff around me like anyone does, especially if there’s a loud noise or someone walks by, but I’m not sure I get pulled off task constantly because of it.\n\nLike, in class or in lab, there are always people moving around, equipment sounds, conversations happening, and I might look over for a second, but I don’t have a clear example where I’m like, “Oh yeah, that completely derailed me all the time.” I guess I’d need to think about it more, because sometimes it’s hard to tell what’s normal distraction and what’s more than that.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like acting on a whim without thinking, when you're alone or not interacting with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when you say “impulsively,” I think of stuff with other people more, like blurting things out or jumping into something socially. When I’m by myself, it’s harder for me to tell what counts.\n\nI mean, I’ll sometimes do things just because the idea pops into my head, like changing what I’m doing suddenly or deciding I’m going to do something right then instead of later, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or if that’s just normal. I don’t have a specific example where I’m like, “Yeah, I constantly act on a whim alone and it causes problems.” It’s not something I’ve really tracked, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t confidently say it happens often when I’m alone. That’s probably the part that scares me most — it’s not just, “Oh, I need to be more professional.” It feels like this could mess up what I’m trying to do career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms, such as difficulty sitting still or interrupting others, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’ll think I’m just moving quickly or jumping in, and then afterward I find out it came across as disruptive or made the team’s rhythm worse.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where I was put on a formal remediation plan, so it feels pretty serious. I’ll think I’m just moving quickly or jumping in, and then afterward I find out it came across as disruptive or made the team’s rhythm worse.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where I was put on a formal remediation plan, so it feels pretty serious. I’m worried that if I don’t get this under control, it could actually cost me my place in the program. That’s been stressful, because this is what I’m trying to build my career around, and I don’t want my behavior to be the reason I can’t continue.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms, such as interrupting others or acting impulsively, caused problems in your personal relationships or friendships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / radiologic_technologist_or_technician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clear example where I can say, like, “This friendship ended because of that,” or anything that obvious.

With work and school stuff, it’s easier for me to point to because people have given me direct feedback. With personal relationships, it’s more… I don’t know, less concrete. I guess I worry sometimes that maybe I come across differently than I mean to, but I can’t say for sure that it’s caused major problems with friends or relationships. If it has, I don’t think anyone has really sat me down and told me that directly.

I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to. It’s not like I’m trying to take over the conversation, but it comes out before I’ve really stopped myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through in situations that don't involve talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as having a big organization system or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d say that’s the main problem.\n\nLike, with school or training stuff, I usually know what I’m supposed to do, but I’d have to think more about whether I’m bad at planning it out or managing time. I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me the way I do with interrupting or waiting my turn. It’s possible I’m missing it, but I haven’t really noticed that as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, my space isn’t like perfectly organized or anything, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major problem, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, my room can get messy in a normal way, and I’ll have clothes or school stuff around sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example where it’s caused some big issue. With a workspace, I guess I try to keep the important stuff where it belongs, especially if other people are using the area too. I don’t really notice myself constantly losing things because of a messy space or being unable to function because of it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe a little messy sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of keeping my living space or workspace organized as one of the main things I struggle with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I notice stuff around me like anyone does, especially if there’s a loud noise or someone walks by, but I’m not sure I get pulled off task constantly because of it.\n\nLike, in class or in lab, there are always people moving around, equipment sounds, conversations happening, and I might look over for a second, but I don’t have a clear example where I’m like, “Oh yeah, that completely derailed me all the time.” I guess I’d need to think about it more, because sometimes it’s hard to tell what’s normal distraction and what’s more than that.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like acting on a whim without thinking, when you're alone or not interacting with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when you say “impulsively,” I think of stuff with other people more, like blurting things out or jumping into something socially. It’s not just boredom, exactly—it’s like this pressure in my body that builds up until I have to move.\n\nI’ll be shifting around constantly, bouncing my leg, standing up when I probably shouldn’t, pacing if I can get away with it, or finding some reason to go grab something or check something just so I’m not stuck sitting there. I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. If a supervisor is explaining a positioning step, or they’re asking me something, I’ll jump in with what I think the answer is before they’re done talking. And then, a second later, I realize I missed the part that actually mattered—like they were adding a detail or correcting something specific, and I already cut them off. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations outside of school or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, outside of school or work it happens too, but the clearest example is still when someone is explaining something to me and I jump in too fast. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to. It’s not like I’m trying to take over the conversation, but it comes out before I’ve really stopped myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through in situations that don't involve talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as having a big organization system or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d say that’s the main problem.\n\nLike, with school or training stuff, I usually know what I’m supposed to do, but I’d have to think more about whether I’m bad at planning it out or managing time. I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me the way I do with interrupting or waiting my turn. It’s possible I’m missing it, but I haven’t really noticed that as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, my space isn’t like perfectly organized or anything, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major problem, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, my room can get messy in a normal way, and I’ll have clothes or school stuff around sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example where it’s caused some big issue. With a workspace, I guess I try to keep the important stuff where it belongs, especially if other people are using the area too. I don’t really notice myself constantly losing things because of a messy space or being unable to function because of it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe a little messy sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of keeping my living space or workspace organized as one of the main things I struggle with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I notice stuff around me like anyone does, especially if there’s a loud noise or someone walks by, but I’m not sure I get pulled off task constantly because of it.\n\nLike, in class or in lab, there are always people moving around, equipment sounds, conversations happening, and I might look over for a second, but I don’t have a clear example where I’m like, “Oh yeah, that completely derailed me all the time.” I guess I’d need to think about it more, because sometimes it’s hard to tell what’s normal distraction and what’s more than that.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like acting on a whim without thinking, when you're alone or not interacting with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when you say “impulsively,” I think of stuff with other people more, like blurting things out or jumping into something socially. When I’m by myself, it’s harder for me to tell what counts.\n\nI mean, I’ll sometimes do things just because the idea pops into my head, like changing what I’m doing suddenly or deciding I’m going to do something right then instead of later, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or if that’s just normal. I don’t have a specific example where I’m like, “Yeah, I constantly act on a whim alone and it causes problems.” It’s not something I’ve really tracked, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t confidently say it happens often when I’m alone. It’s possible I’m missing it, but I haven’t really noticed that as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, my space isn’t like perfectly organized or anything, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major problem, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, my room can get messy in a normal way, and I’ll have clothes or school stuff around sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example where it’s caused some big issue. With a workspace, I guess I try to keep the important stuff where it belongs, especially if other people are using the area too. I don’t really notice myself constantly losing things because of a messy space or being unable to function because of it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe a little messy sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of keeping my living space or workspace organized as one of the main things I struggle with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I notice stuff around me like anyone does, especially if there’s a loud noise or someone walks by, but I’m not sure I get pulled off task constantly because of it.\n\nLike, in class or in lab, there are always people moving around, equipment sounds, conversations happening, and I might look over for a second, but I don’t have a clear example where I’m like, “Oh yeah, that completely derailed me all the time.” I guess I’d need to think about it more, because sometimes it’s hard to tell what’s normal distraction and what’s more than that.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like acting on a whim without thinking, when you're alone or not interacting with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when you say “impulsively,” I think of stuff with other people more, like blurting things out or jumping into something socially. When I’m by myself, it’s harder for me to tell what counts.\n\nI mean, I’ll sometimes do things just because the idea pops into my head, like changing what I’m doing suddenly or deciding I’m going to do something right then instead of later, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or if that’s just normal. I don’t have a specific example where I’m like, “Yeah, I constantly act on a whim alone and it causes problems.” It’s not something I’ve really tracked, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t confidently say it happens often when I’m alone. I’d probably need you to give me examples of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as difficulty sitting still or interrupting others, caused you any significant problems in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s actually where it’s been the biggest problem, honestly. It’s not just annoying or something people comment on casually — it’s started affecting my program in a real way.\n\nMy clinical evaluations have gone down, and my supervisors have told me that the way I act during clinicals can disrupt the flow with patients and make teamwork harder. That was kind of hard to hear, because I don’t always realize in the moment how much I’m affecting the room or the pace of things. But it’s gotten serious enough that I was put on a formal remediation plan.\n\nAnd I’m pretty worried about it, because it feels like if I don’t get it under control, it could actually cost me my spot in the program. That’s probably the part that scares me most — it’s not just, “Oh, I need to be more professional.” It feels like this could mess up what I’m trying to do career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms, such as difficulty sitting still or interrupting others, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not just annoying or something people comment on casually — it’s started affecting my program in a real way.\n\nMy clinical evaluations have gone down, and my supervisors have told me that the way I act during clinicals can disrupt the flow with patients and make teamwork harder. That was kind of hard to hear, because I don’t always realize in the moment how much I’m affecting the room or the pace of things. But it’s gotten serious enough that I was put on a formal remediation plan.\n\nAnd I’m pretty worried about it, because it feels like if I don’t get it under control, it could actually cost me my spot in the program. That’s probably the part that scares me most — it’s not just, “Oh, I need to be more professional.” It feels like this could mess up what I’m trying to do career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms, such as difficulty sitting still or interrupting others, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the reason I say yes is because my parents still bring up stuff from when I was really little, like elementary school parent-teacher conferences. They’ve told me more than once that teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling things out, or starting ahead before the teacher had even finished giving directions.\n\nAnd that was way before any of this radiology program stress or clinical training stuff. Like, it wasn’t just something that showed up recently because I’m under pressure. My parents kind of talk about it like it was a pattern even back then, before middle school. I don’t remember every detail myself because I was a kid, but I remember enough of the general feeling of being the kid who was always being corrected, and my parents definitely remember the conferences.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms, such as restlessness or interrupting others, have caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, that’s probably the part that’s making me take it seriously now, because it’s not just me feeling restless or people being mildly annoyed with me. It’s actually affecting whether I can function in my program the way I’m supposed to.\n\nMy clinical evaluations have taken a hit, and that really got my attention. My supervisors have basically said that some of my behavior during clinicals disrupts patient flow and makes teamwork harder, which is honestly embarrassing to hear because I’m not trying to make things harder for anyone. I’ll think I’m just moving quickly or jumping in, and then afterward I find out it came across as disruptive or made the team’s rhythm worse.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where I was put on a formal remediation plan, so it feels pretty serious. I’m worried that if I don’t get this under control, it could actually cost me my place in the program. That’s been stressful, because this is what I’m trying to build my career around, and I don’t want my behavior to be the reason I can’t continue.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as interrupting others or acting impulsively, caused problems in your personal relationships or friendships?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clear example where I can say, like, “This friendship ended because of that,” or anything that obvious.\n\nWith work and school stuff, it’s easier for me to point to because people have given me direct feedback. With personal relationships, it’s more… I don’t know, less concrete. I’m worried it could cost me my place in the program if I don’t get it under control.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. I mean, that’s actually where it feels the most serious right now. It’s not just like, “Oh, I’m annoyed with myself because I didn’t finish something.” It’s affecting my program and the goals I’m trying to work toward.

My clinical evaluations have definitely taken a hit. I’ve gotten feedback from supervisors that the way I’m acting is disrupting patient flow and making it harder for the team to work smoothly. And that’s really stressful, because in that setting everything has to move in a certain order and people are depending on you, so when I mess that up, it’s not just my own problem.

They actually put me on a formal remediation plan, which was kind of a wake-up call. Like, it made it feel official that this isn’t just me being hard on myself. Other people are seeing it, and it’s affecting how I’m being evaluated. I’m honestly worried that if I don’t get it under control, it could cost me my place in the program, which is scary because this is what I’m trying to build my future around.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I just can’t sit still, and it’s getting really hard to keep pretending I can. Like, when I’m in the radiology suite or even in class, if I’m supposed to be sitting there quietly or staying in one spot, I feel like I’m crawling out of my skin. It’s not just boredom, exactly—it’s like this pressure in my body that builds up until I have to move.\n\nI’ll be shifting around constantly, bouncing my leg, standing up when I probably shouldn’t, pacing if I can get away with it, or finding some reason to go grab something or check something just so I’m not stuck sitting there. I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t remember every single moment from being that young, obviously, but my parents still bring it up like it was this recurring thing. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. So it’s not like this just suddenly showed up because my current schedule is hard or because I’m in a more structured program. It was apparently a thing even back when I was little, like in elementary school, where sitting still and waiting my turn was already a problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did, and I still do it now, honestly. Like, in school I’d blurt stuff out before the teacher had even finished the question, and I wouldn’t always realize I was doing it until people reacted.\n\nNow it happens in training too. If a supervisor is explaining a positioning step, or they’re asking me something, I’ll jump in with what I think the answer is before they’re done talking. And then, a second later, I realize I missed the part that actually mattered—like they were adding a detail or correcting something specific, and I already cut them off. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations outside of school or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, outside of school or work it happens too, but the clearest example is still when someone is explaining something to me and I jump in too fast. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to. It’s not like I’m trying to take over the conversation, but it comes out before I’ve really stopped myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through in situations that don't involve talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The main thing that comes to mind is waiting my turn during lab practice, or even just waiting for a machine to open up. I get really edgy, like physically uncomfortable almost, and I’ll keep stepping forward a little, checking the time, looking around, trying to see if the person is almost done.\n\nAnd then I’ll ask, like, “Can I go next?” or “Are you almost finished?” even when it’s probably obvious I’m being pushy. It’s not that I sit there and calmly think, “This is a good idea.” It’s more like I feel this pressure to move, to do something, and then I’m already asking before I’ve really thought through how annoying it might come across.\n\nI’ve been called out for it more than once, too. Like people will say, “You need to wait,” or “We’re going in order,” and then I feel embarrassed because I know they’re right. I’m not trying to cut people off or act like I’m more important, but in the moment waiting feels way harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as having a big organization system or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d say that’s the main problem.\n\nLike, with school or training stuff, I usually know what I’m supposed to do, but I’d have to think more about whether I’m bad at planning it out or managing time. I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me the way I do with interrupting or waiting my turn. It’s possible I’m missing it, but I haven’t really noticed that as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, my space isn’t like perfectly organized or anything, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major problem, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, my room can get messy in a normal way, and I’ll have clothes or school stuff around sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example where it’s caused some big issue. With a workspace, I guess I try to keep the important stuff where it belongs, especially if other people are using the area too. I don’t really notice myself constantly losing things because of a messy space or being unable to function because of it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe a little messy sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of keeping my living space or workspace organized as one of the main things I struggle with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I notice stuff around me like anyone does, especially if there’s a loud noise or someone walks by, but I’m not sure I get pulled off task constantly because of it.\n\nLike, in class or in lab, there are always people moving around, equipment sounds, conversations happening, and I might look over for a second, but I don’t have a clear example where I’m like, “Oh yeah, that completely derailed me all the time.” I guess I’d need to think about it more, because sometimes it’s hard to tell what’s normal distraction and what’s more than that.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like acting on a whim without thinking, when you're alone or not interacting with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because when you say “impulsively,” I think of stuff with other people more, like blurting things out or jumping into something socially. When I’m by myself, it’s harder for me to tell what counts.\n\nI mean, I’ll sometimes do things just because the idea pops into my head, like changing what I’m doing suddenly or deciding I’m going to do something right then instead of later, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or if that’s just normal. I don’t have a specific example where I’m like, “Yeah, I constantly act on a whim alone and it causes problems.” It’s not something I’ve really tracked, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t confidently say it happens often when I’m alone. I’d probably need you to give me examples of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as difficulty sitting still or interrupting others, caused you any significant problems in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s actually where it’s been the biggest problem, honestly. It’s not just annoying or something people comment on casually — it’s started affecting my program in a real way.\n\nMy clinical evaluations have gone down, and my supervisors have told me that the way I act during clinicals can disrupt the flow with patients and make teamwork harder. That was kind of hard to hear, because I don’t always realize in the moment how much I’m affecting the room or the pace of things. But it’s gotten serious enough that I was put on a formal remediation plan.\n\nAnd I’m pretty worried about it, because it feels like if I don’t get it under control, it could actually cost me my spot in the program. That’s probably the part that scares me most — it’s not just, “Oh, I need to be more professional.” It feels like this could mess up what I’m trying to do career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms, such as difficulty sitting still or interrupting others, consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the reason I say yes is because my parents still bring up stuff from when I was really little, like elementary school parent-teacher conferences. They’ve told me more than once that teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling things out, or starting ahead before the teacher had even finished giving directions.\n\nAnd that was way before any of this radiology program stress or clinical training stuff. Like, it wasn’t just something that showed up recently because I’m under pressure. My parents kind of talk about it like it was a pattern even back then, before middle school. I don’t remember every detail myself because I was a kid, but I remember enough of the general feeling of being the kid who was always being corrected, and my parents definitely remember the conferences.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms, such as restlessness or interrupting others, have caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, that’s probably the part that’s making me take it seriously now, because it’s not just me feeling restless or people being mildly annoyed with me. It’s actually affecting whether I can function in my program the way I’m supposed to.\n\nMy clinical evaluations have taken a hit, and that really got my attention. My supervisors have basically said that some of my behavior during clinicals disrupts patient flow and makes teamwork harder, which is honestly embarrassing to hear because I’m not trying to make things harder for anyone. I’ll think I’m just moving quickly or jumping in, and then afterward I find out it came across as disruptive or made the team’s rhythm worse.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where I was put on a formal remediation plan, so it feels pretty serious. I’m worried that if I don’t get this under control, it could actually cost me my place in the program. That’s been stressful, because this is what I’m trying to build my career around, and I don’t want my behavior to be the reason I can’t continue.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as interrupting others or acting impulsively, caused problems in your personal relationships or friendships?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clear example where I can say, like, “This friendship ended because of that,” or anything that obvious.\n\nWith work and school stuff, it’s easier for me to point to because people have given me direct feedback. With personal relationships, it’s more… I don’t know, less concrete. I guess I worry sometimes that maybe I come across differently than I mean to, but I can’t say for sure that it’s caused major problems with friends or relationships. If it has, I don’t think anyone has really sat me down and told me that directly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a friend or had a friendship become strained because of your behavior?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not in a clear way. Like, I can’t point to a specific friendship and say, “Yeah, that ended because I interrupted too much,” or “that person stopped talking to me because I acted impulsively.” Nothing like that has really been said to me directly.\n\nI guess sometimes I wonder if people get tired of me or if I’m more intense than I realize, but that’s more me worrying after the fact than something I actually know happened. With friends, it’s hard to tell because people don’t usually give you a formal evaluation or sit you down and explain exactly what bothered them. So I don’t want to say no like it’s impossible, but I also don’t have an example of losing a friend or a friendship becoming clearly strained because of my behavior.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your behavior has strained or damaged a personal relationship?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Not in a way where I can say, “Yes, this relationship was damaged because of how I acted.” I don’t have a specific person in mind where there was a big argument or someone told me, “You’re too much,” or “You keep interrupting me,” or anything that direct.\n\nI do sometimes look back on conversations and wonder if I came off wrong, or if I was talking over someone without meaning to, but I don’t know if that actually strained the relationship or if I’m just overthinking it afterward. With personal stuff, it’s harder for me to measure because people don’t always give clear feedback. So I guess my answer is: maybe it could have, but I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern or had someone tell me directly that I damaged a personal relationship that way.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, that’s actually where it feels the most serious right now. It’s not just like, “Oh, I’m annoyed with myself because I didn’t finish something.” It’s affecting my program and the goals I’m trying to work toward.\n\nMy clinical evaluations have definitely taken a hit. I’ve gotten feedback from supervisors that the way I’m acting is disrupting patient flow and making it harder for the team to work smoothly. And that’s really stressful, because in that setting everything has to move in a certain order and people are depending on you, so when I mess that up, it’s not just my own problem.\n\nThey actually put me on a formal remediation plan, which was kind of a wake-up call. Like, it made it feel official that this isn’t just me being hard on myself. Other people are seeing it, and it’s affecting how I’m being evaluated. I’m honestly worried that if I don’t get it under control, it could cost me my place in the program, which is scary because this is what I’m trying to build my future around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I’ve had relationships where things didn’t work out, but I can’t honestly say, “That ended because of my behavior,” or “there was this major problem caused by the way I acted.” Nobody has ever sat me down and said that was the reason, at least not in a way I can point to.\n\nI do sometimes replay things afterward and wonder if I was too much, or if I handled something badly, or if I missed what the other person needed in the moment. But that’s more me second-guessing myself than knowing for sure. Romantic relationships can be kind of complicated anyway, so I don’t want to blame everything on myself if I don’t actually know that’s what happened.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: maybe it’s possible, but I haven’t noticed a definite pattern, and I don’t have a specific relationship where I know my behavior ended it or caused a major problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you consistently experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or play activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / radiologic_technologist_or_technician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that one, because I don’t know if I’d describe my main problem as “inattention” exactly. Like, I can lose focus sometimes, sure, but I don’t have a clear sense that it’s this consistent, major thing across everything I do.

If I’m doing something that’s interesting or that has immediate pressure, I can usually stay with it, at least enough to get through it. But if it’s something slower or more repetitive, I might drift a bit or need to bring myself back. I don’t know if that’s more than what other people experience, though.

So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed significant, consistent inattention in the way you’re asking. Or at least I’m not confident enough to say yes. I’d probably need you to clarify what level of difficulty you mean, because I don’t want to overstate it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I just can’t sit still, and it’s getting really hard to keep pretending I can. It’s not just boredom, exactly—it’s like this pressure in my body that builds up until I have to move.\n\nI’ll be shifting around constantly, bouncing my leg, standing up when I probably shouldn’t, pacing if I can get away with it, or finding some reason to go grab something or check something just so I’m not stuck sitting there. I’ve been trying for months to just tough it out and tell myself to calm down, but it keeps happening, and it’s starting to feel like I’m fighting myself all day.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or inability to sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t remember every single moment from being that young, obviously, but my parents still bring it up like it was this recurring thing. They’ll talk about elementary school parent-teacher conferences where the teachers would say I was always getting out of my seat, or calling out answers, or starting on stuff before they’d even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was way before middle school or anything like my radiology training now. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be rude, it just comes out before I’ve really stopped to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations outside of school or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, outside of school or work it happens too, but the clearest example is still when someone is explaining something to me and I jump in too fast. Like if a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll answer before they’re even finished, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling socially, where my mouth gets ahead of me. I think I know where the person is going, so I start responding, and then halfway through I realize they weren’t done or there was another detail I didn’t let them get to.
